### Sprint 22 — Load testing + SLO calibration (GA hardening — Epic D) — TODO

**Goal:** Turn the observability stack from wired to *trusted*. `deploy/prometheus-rules.yml`
used to say of itself that every threshold except §6's <1s p99 was "a conservative placeholder
picked with no production traffic to calibrate against". Until they are validated,
the alerting is decorative: thresholds nobody measured produce either noise or silence,
and both teach an on-call to ignore the page.

t1 and t2 have closed that gap for the chain and the API, and the answer turned out to be
worse than "untuned": four rules could not fire at all, and the K8s deployment was running
a *different, staler* rule set than the one under review. Both are now enforced rather than
asserted — `crates/alert-conformance` fails the build on an unfireable rule, and Kubernetes
generates its rules from the same file the compose stack loads. **What remains uncalibrated
is scoped and stated:** the load test drives the chain and the API only, so nothing in the
simulation, embedding, clustering, copilot or notification groups is measurement-backed —
those rules rest on an exported gauge or a structural condition and say so. t3–t5 below are
the rest of the epic.

| # | Task | Est | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Load test at target throughput** (D/line 81): chain tps, peak alert volume, API qps. Verify the §6 fast path holds < 1s p99 **under load**, not in isolation — the claim is the platform's headline latency number and has never been measured against a saturated pipeline | 10h | DONE (2026-09-07) — **the claim had no series behind it, so the instrument came first.** Detection's only latency metric was `detector_detect_duration_seconds`: one `detect` call, microseconds on a header-only block and *arithmetically unaffected* by a queue in front of it — and `prometheus-rules.yml`'s `FastPathLatencyHigh` was reading exactly that, so the alert guarding the headline number could not fire on the failure it names. [`detection::metrics::FAST_PATH_SECONDS`](../../crates/detection/src/metrics.rs) now spans `BlockAssembled.occurred_at` → durable publication (**across the broker hop and the scheduler's bounded work channel**), split into `detection_queue_wait_seconds` + `detection_block_process_seconds` so a breach says *which* term grew; clock anchored to the producer's timestamp, `BlockReverted` deliberately carries none, recorded through a §14 `handle_one`/`_inner` wrapper. [`crates/loadtest/`](../../crates/loadtest) offers the load: blocks stamped with the time they were **due, not sent** (a generator that slows down with the system is coordinated omission and reports a latency that never happened); the run **drains before measuring** (queued blocks are the slow ones); the window is a **difference of two scrapes**; and the verdict is **three-way** — `0` held / `1` breached / `2` undecided, because a load test has more ways to be uninformative than to fail. A bucketed quantile is a **bound**: `≥99% at or below the budget`, decidable exactly on the ladder's 1.0 boundary, and a budget off the ladder is rejected when `slo.json` loads. Drivers sit behind a `LoadSource`/`SubjectMetrics` seam (§2) so the *procedure* is tested against in-memory doubles; [`gates.rs`](../../crates/loadtest/src/gates.rs) is a fail-closed registry whose test asserts every `slo.json` field is read by some rule (which is what makes t3's screening p50 un-forgettable). Load lives in reviewable `profiles/*.json`, budgets in `slo.json` with **no `--update` flag**. `just load-test{,-smoke,-headroom}`, nightly [`load-test.yml`](../../.github/workflows/load-test.yml) where **exit 2 fails the job**, runbook [`docs/runbooks/load-test.md`](../../docs/runbooks/load-test.md), arch-conformance rule (no store/service edge), conventions **§19**. **Measured:** `mainnet-peak` (4 blocks/s, 1001 declared tps, 1200 alerting samples) held at p99 ≤ 250ms with the API idle; with 100 qps co-resident, queue wait went 2.5ms → 500ms and the fast path 250ms → **1.000s** — a 4× degradation landing on the budget boundary, invisible to the old series. *Not a capacity number* (five services on one laptop); a green run on hardware that holds a broker up is still owed, and is what t2 calibrates against. |
| 2 | **Calibrate every provisional threshold** in `prometheus-rules.yml` against those numbers (D/line 79), and delete the `severity: informational` placeholders that no longer earn their place. Includes the Sprint 20 t5 additions: the grounding-audit and token-budget alarms have never fired against real volume either | 5h | DONE (2026-09-11) — **the calibration found almost no mis-tuned numbers; it found rules that could not fire.** Four of them, and the reason they survived review is the defect class itself: *an alert has no failing state.* A test reddens, a pod crash-loops, a type stops compiling — a rule whose threshold is unreachable is indistinguishable from a healthy system, and green is what you were hoping to see. **The ceiling bug (×2).** Every `_seconds` histogram shared one ladder topping out at **10s**, and `histogram_quantile` reports the highest *finite* bound for a quantile in `+Inf` — so `AlertEndToEndLatencyHigh` (`> 30`) and `EmbeddingSweepLapTooSlow` (`> 21600`) were capped at 10 and had been green since the day they were written, the same shape as t1's `FastPathLatencyHigh`. **The cadence bug (×4).** The grounding-audit rules read a **weekly** CronJob through `increase(...[1d])`: blind six days in seven, and `increase()` needs two samples where a short-lived Job may be scraped once → `max_over_time(...[8d])`. **The vacuity bug (×2).** `usage::budget::run` returns immediately when `USAGE_TOKEN_BUDGET` is unset (the shipped default), exporting *no* series — and absence satisfies a threshold rule, so the alarm **and** its own monitor-the-monitor rule were both green for the same reason. Genuinely calibrated from t1's numbers: `FastPathQueueWaitHigh` 0.5 → **0.25** (0.5 was *exactly* the queue wait measured when the fast path hit its 1s ceiling, so the "leading indicator" led by nothing; healthy is 2.5ms), `ApiLatencyHigh` 1.0 → **0.5** to match `slo.json`'s `api_p99_seconds` (the run breached that budget while the alert sat green on a threshold twice as loose), a new **`ApiErrorRatioHigh`** for the measured 0.883 success ratio that nothing was watching, and `DetectorLatencyBudgetExceeded` 0.25 → **0.05** (250ms was the *entire* measured healthy fast path). Six `informational` placeholders **deleted** — each an invented number attached to an *observation* rather than a fault, which no amount of traffic would ever have turned into an SLO — five **promoted to `warning`** (structural all along, merely mislabeled), one survivor whose severity is argued rather than unpicked. **The most serious finding was not in this file.** `deploy/k8s/base/infra/prometheus.yaml` carried an *inlined copy* of the rules that had drifted for three sprints: missing whole groups, and its `FastPathLatencyHigh` still pointed at `detector_detect_duration_seconds` — t1's fix landed in the compose file and never in the one that runs in production. K8s now **generates** its ConfigMap from `deploy/prometheus-rules.yml`, as the Grafana dashboards already do for exactly this reason. **Then the contract became a test.** All of the above was prose at the top of a YAML file, which is how it got there: [`crates/alert-conformance`](../../crates/alert-conformance/) (sibling of `arch-conformance`) now fails `cargo test` on an off-ladder or above-ceiling threshold, a batch series read through too narrow a window or the wrong function, an unroutable severity, a **new** `informational` rule, a missing annotation, or a duplicate name — verified by re-introducing the shipped `> 30` bug. The root cause is fixed too: `telemetry::metrics` gained `bucket_class`/`buckets_for` and a second **`JOB_DURATION_BUCKETS_SECONDS`** ladder (1s–1 day), because `_seconds` does not distinguish "how long did this call take" from "how long until this comes round again" — **three more metrics beyond the two alerts were silently pinned at 10s, including `predictive_liquidation_lead_time_seconds`, the §19 signal for the predictive pipeline's headline claim** (`_sum`/`_count` were always exact, so the *mean* was right; only quantiles lied, which is worse than an error because 10.0 looks like an answer). One definition, three readers — exporter, `loadtest`, conformance — with a test pinning that `Matcher::Full` really beats the `_seconds` suffix in rendered output, and a source scan catching a typo'd metric name (a `Matcher::Full` that matches nothing fails silently). Two smaller repairs: the disarmed-budget rule reads an explicit `usage_token_budget_enabled` gauge published *before* the disabled-path return rather than inferring "off" from `absent()` (**conventions §15b, "declare your arming state"**), and `loadtest`'s budgets became a `LatencyBudget` newtype validated inside `Deserialize`, so an off-ladder budget can no longer be *parsed*. Conventions **§19b**. |
| 3 | **Screening API critical-path SLO** (D/line 80): `/screen` sits inline on customer withdrawals, so its p50 < 100ms is **contractual, not aesthetic**. Bounded p99 budget under load, plus the graceful degradation the doc asks for — serve a stale-but-flagged cached score rather than block a customer's withdrawal when intelligence is slow | 8h | DONE (2026-09-13) — **degradation widens what `/screen` can answer; it never turns "could not decide" into "allow".** *Stale-but-flagged fallback* ([`server::degrade`](../../crates/server/src/degrade.rs)): every fresh `GetScreeningFacts` reply is snapshotted to Redis off the request path; past the fresh budget — or on a *transient* fault — a snapshot no older than `SCREENING_STALE_MAX_AGE_SECS` answers, runs through the caller's current policy, and discloses `stale: true` + reason + age on the response **and** `ScreeningDecisionRecorded.facts_staleness` (one `ScreeningOutcome` derives both). No usable snapshot is still a 502; permanent faults never degrade. *The p50 contract under an incident* ([`server::facts_source`](../../crates/server/src/facts_source.rs)): bulkhead → circuit breaker → latency tracker → hedged reads. The breaker counts slow and **abandoned** reads (cancellation is an outcome, recorded on drop), so a persistently slow intelligence opens it and snapshots answer in one read instead of every call waiting the budget; hedges are token-budgeted; the fresh budget follows the recent p99 between a floor and ceiling. *Substrate*: a per-pod L1 snapshot tier with digest write-skipping ([`server::snapshot_tier`](../../crates/server/src/snapshot_tier.rs)), because Redis shares a failure domain with intelligence's cache. *The legal gap*: sanctions never come from a snapshot — a pod-local sanctions view ([`server::sanctions_view`](../../crates/server/src/sanctions_view.rs)) over a new keyset-paged `ListSanctions` RPC + watermark is merged into every decision, and a stale `allow` it cannot vouch for is held as `review`. Customers choose `on_stale: serve | review` per versioned policy (migration `20260913000000`; `strict` holds; new `StaleFactsReview` basis). *Budgets + proof*: `slo.json` `screen_p50_seconds: 0.1` / `screen_p99_seconds: 0.25` judged per route over 2xx `/screen` only; `max_screen_failed_closed_share: 0.01` judged by `profiles/screening-degraded.json` (`just load-test-degraded`: 300ms injected into intelligence through a harness-owned delay-line proxy for the measurement window only, over a bounded address pool), inconclusive when the fault never reached the screening path. 1 KiB `/screen` payload cap. *Alerts*: `ScreeningLatencyP99High`, `ScreeningFailingClosed` (page), `ScreeningServingStaleFacts`, `ScreeningSnapshotWritesLost`, `ScreeningDegradationDisarmed`, `ScreeningIntelligenceBreakerOpen`, `ScreeningSanctionsViewStale`, `ScreeningBulkheadRejecting`. *Found building it*: the schema gate called required fields inside a *new optional object* breaking — refined (`newly_added_ancestor`, SCHEMA.md) rather than faking serde defaults on the disclosure. **Still open:** budgets declared, not yet measured against a real stack; delisting is not modelled (the sanctions store only upserts, so the view errs toward blocking); hedging to a different pod behind a ClusterIP is probabilistic |
| 4 | **Autoscaling on the designed backpressure signals** (D/line 82): simulation workers on `sim.jobs` queue depth (§7's own signal), HPA on detection/api. Deliberately *not* the copilot — what reaches the provider is `replicas x concurrency` against an org-wide limit, so that stays a spend decision an operator makes | 6h | DONE (2026-09-14) — **the worker pool now scales on the signal §7 designed for it, and making that safe exposed three bugs worse than the missing feature.** *Signal*: the worker exports `simulation_worker_job_capacity` (workers × prefetch, a `NonZero` `JobCapacity` — a prefetch of 0 is AMQP for *unlimited*, and a capacity of 0 divides the rule into +Inf); the recording rule `simulation:sim_jobs_replicas_demanded` = (ready + unacked) ÷ capacity feeds the HPA as an external metric through a hand-rolled prometheus-adapter in [`deploy/k8s/cluster/`](../../deploy/k8s/cluster/) (apart from base: its APIService is cluster-wide, and kustomize 5.x rewrites its `kube-system` RoleBinding) against `AverageValue: 1`, so the scaling point is each replica's own declared bound rather than a literal. Scale-down is paced (1 pod/min), and the HPA holds capacity when the signal is unreadable. `sim.jobs` is bounded (`x-max-length-bytes` 100 MiB, `x-overflow: reject-publish`): a nack becomes the dispatcher's `Handled::Retry`, so at maxReplicas the backlog waits in Kafka lag, not broker memory. *Detection: no HPA, deliberately* — the chain is the partition key, so a second replica rebalances the one partition and then idles; the lever is isolation: Guaranteed QoS (requests == limits, including the ML component and its init container) plus a preferred anti-affinity keeping autoscaled API pods off detection's nodes, which is what the load test showed degrading it. *API*: CPU only with paced scale-down (each removal reconnects that pod's WebSocket clients) — no latency input (more pods load a slow intelligence further), no qps input until staging measures per-pod throughput. *Bugs found*: **no pod could have started in any cluster** — `deploy/Dockerfile` ended on `USER appuser` (a name) under `runAsNonRoot: true`, which the kubelet only verifies against a number, as were Prometheus/Alertmanager (`nobody`); now numeric and enforced by [`arch-conformance/tests/deploy_users.rs`](../../crates/arch-conformance/tests/deploy_users.rs). **`Handled::Retry` skipped the record it meant to retry** — `run_consumer` went back to `recv()`, which hands the next record, so the next `Commit` passed the retried one; it now seeks back, proven against a real broker by [`event-bus/tests/retry_refetch.rs`](../../crates/event-bus/tests/retry_refetch.rs). **Delivery was guessed from the shutdown token** — the worker requeued jobs whose results had landed (re-running revm and re-metering on every scale-down) and detection committed past alerts it had not published; `publish_resilient` now returns `Result<(), Undelivered>` (counted by `event_publish_abandoned_total`), consumers settle on it, and fire-and-forget callers write `.accept_loss(why)`. *Grace period, derived*: a per-job deadline (`SIMULATION_JOB_DEADLINE_SECS`, 45s: resolver timed out, revm checked between transactions) makes `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` computable — 45 + 5s overshoot + 2 results × 30s send timeout = 110s ≤ 120s, asserted against the manifest by [`simulation/tests/grace_period.rs`](../../crates/simulation/tests/grace_period.rs); [`deploy_scaling.rs`](../../crates/arch-conformance/tests/deploy_scaling.rs) fails the build on an HPA over a `Recreate` workload, a dangling target, or a hard-coded `replicas` beside an HPA (all three targets had one). *Alerts*: `SimulationBacklogOutgrowingWorkers`, `SimulationQueueSignalDown`, `SimulationQueueAtBound`, `SimulationJobsMissingDeadline`, `SimulationDeadlineOvershootAboveAllowance`, `EventPublishPermanentlyAbandoned`; conventions §12 gained four rules. **Demonstrated on kind** (real RabbitMQ, Prometheus, rules and adapter; stub worker container): 300 jobs → the rule read 4.67 → **2 → 4 → 5 replicas in 37s**; drained → **5 → 4 → 3 → 2** at exactly the 300s window plus 60s steps; a real broker nacks at the bound (`tests/topology.rs`). **Still open:** the per-pod API qps input waits on the staging run; the demo used a stub, not the real worker image; the correlator's finality tracker releases a finding before its retraction is published (a counted, stated gap) |
| 5 | **Capacity plan** (D/line 84): partition counts, shard counts, and storage growth for an event store that grows forever. Model the cost before it is a surprise, not after | 4h | TODO |

**Deliverable:** Every alert threshold is a measured number with a load-test run behind it; the fast path and `/screen` have SLOs proven under saturation; and the storage bill has a curve rather than a guess.

### Sprint 23 — Detection quality: the FP-rate claim (GA hardening — Epic E) — TODO

**Goal:** Make the accuracy claim true or stop making it. The README targets **< 4% false
positives**; the promotion gate replays **8 curated fixtures** (7 scenarios + 1 clean block)
at precision 1.0. That is a regression signal, not a field measurement — a single negative
block cannot resolve a 4% target. Everything needed to close this already exists
(`dataset` export, the simulation-confirmation flywheel, `backtest` replay), which makes it
the best credibility-per-hour on the whole list.

| # | Task | Est | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **A fixture set adequate to support the claim** (E/line 94): adversarial negatives plus replayed mainnet windows, labelled from simulation confirmations (the flywheel Sprint 18 built for exactly this). Until it exists, the README states a *target* and must say so | 10h | TODO |
| 2 | **Key the promotion gate on the full `(id, version, config_hash)` triple** (E/line 93): today `baseline.json` and `model_performance.json` key on detector **id alone**, so a config change that moves precision is indistinguishable from a regression of the same detector — the gate cannot tell "we changed it" from "it broke" | 4h | TODO |
| 3 | **False-positive feedback loop** wired to the §19 FP panel and tracked as an SLO (E/line 96), so the number is monitored in production rather than asserted at merge | 6h | TODO |
| 4 | **Sanctions freshness SLA** (E/line 97): OFAC/EU lists ingested within a bounded window, with staleness alerting — `SanctionHit` is a hard alert (§8.5), so a stale list is a compliance failure that currently fails silently | 4h | TODO |

**Deliverable:** The README's FP number is a measurement with a corpus behind it, or it is labelled a target; and a detector config change can no longer masquerade as a regression.

bytecode the platform ingests by design, and a cross-tenant leak in the rule engine is the
one bug that ends a compliance product.

| 2 | **Multi-tenant isolation proof** (C/line 67): §9's "no cross-customer data leakage" must be a test, not a sentence — a tenant's rule cannot read another tenant's data or events, proven adversarially rather than by reading the owner-from-JWT code and believing it | 6h | TODO |

| 5 | **Access auditing** (C/line 70): log who queried the audit log. The audit trail needs an audit trail — a reviewer reading a SAR narrative is itself a compliance-relevant act | 4h | TODO |
| 6 | **Third-party security review / pen test** before GA (C/line 71). External, because every internal reviewer here shares the same blind spots as the author | — | TODO |

**Deliverable:** The isolation and sandbox guarantees have adversarial tests rather than doc comments, secrets rotate, and an external party has tried to break it.

### Sprint 25 — Reliability, chaos & DLQ operations (GA hardening — Epic A) — TODO

**Goal:** Prove the failure behaviour that the whole architecture was shaped around. Every
consumer claims idempotency, every projection claims reorg-safety, every seam claims to fail
open — under fault injection, not under a unit test that supplies the fault politely.

| # | Task | Est | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Chaos / fault-injection harness** (A/line 40): kill workers mid-revm, drop Kafka brokers, partition the network, replay duplicate and out-of-order events, force deep reorgs — then assert projections converge to the correct state. This is where the §7/§15 claims are either true or discovered false | 10h | TODO |
| 2 | **HA for every stateful component** (A/line 41): Kafka RF ≥3, RabbitMQ quorum queues, replicated ClickHouse, Postgres primary + sync replica with automated failover, Redis HA. The K8s tree currently deploys single instances — fine for a demo, not for a claim | 8h | TODO |
| 3 | **Graceful degradation, tested** (A/line 42): simulation backlog must not stop provisional alerts (the §6 fast/slow split's entire purpose), and a dead intelligence service must degrade the API to stale-but-flagged rather than to an error | 6h | TODO |
| 4 | **DLQ operations** (A/line 44): drain + replay runbook for `sim.jobs.dlx` and every `mev.dlq.*` topic, with depth alerting. Records are parked today and nobody has rehearsed getting them back | 5h | TODO |
| 5 | **Finish the `resilience` retrofit** (A/line 43): the four hand-rolled retry loops in `notification` (http, email) and `rule_engine` (webhook) are the crate's natural adopters and were deliberately left un-migrated in Sprint 20 t1. Two ways to do retries is one way too many | 3h | TODO |
| 6 | **Un-defer simulation fork-from-chain:** Sprint 12 t3 shipped a real revm engine but stubbed fork-from-chain (revm 41's alloydb pins alloy-provider 2.0 against the workspace's 1.x) and left `JobResolver` awaiting a by-alert event-store query. Simulation confirmation is what makes a detection evidence-backed *and* is the label source for Sprint 23's corpus, so the stub weakens two things at once | 8h | TODO |

**Deliverable:** The system has been broken deliberately in every way the design anticipates, and the projections came back correct — with a rehearsed runbook for each.

## Cross-cutting tracks (every sprint, not a separate phase)

- **Observability (§19):** add Prometheus metrics + tracing spans for each new service as it's built; don't backfill.
- **Reorg safety (§15):** any sprint that introduces derived state must handle `BlockReverted` in the same sprint.
- **Event sourcing discipline (§2):** commands stay off the event store; only results re-enter as domain events. Guard this at code review.
- **Idempotent projections (§7):** every consumer dedups replayed events from day one.
- **File size discipline:** keep modules small and single-purpose — split before they sprawl. Line counts below are *guidance, not a gate*: a file in the warning zone is a prompt to ask "is this doing one thing?", not an automatic refactor. Comments/docs and tests don't count against the budget.also need to write api end points to test by usinng postman or swagger.

| Type | Comfortable | Warning zone |
|---|---|---|
| Rust module | ~300 | 500+ |
| Service / handler | ~200 | 400+ |
| Config / constants | ~500 | fine |

## Key risks to watch

1. **Schema churn** — if the §2 event schema changes after Sprint 1, every downstream service ripples. Lock it hard; version explicitly.
2. **Hub-node graph collapse** (§8.2) — the single most likely correctness bug in the moat. Degree caps are not optional.
3. **revm as hostile-input sandbox** (§7) — honeypot bytecode *will* crash workers; redelivery + DLX must work before trusting throughput numbers.
4. **Solo bus factor** — keep each sprint's slice independently demoable so progress is never blocked on a half-finished service.
